If we instead have each block handle a tile of query positions (e.g., 8 or 16) and have threads within the block compute scores for these queries against the same K/V, we can reuse K/V in shared memory, reducing global memory reads. This is the standard flash attention approach. But implementing a full flash attention tile is more complex. However, maybe the naive PyTorch reference is also memory bound and does similar traffic. The PyTorch implementation computes scores = Q @ K^T, which is a batched matrix multiply. cuBLAS will tile this efficiently, loading K into shared memory and reusing it across query positions within a tile. So it's highly optimized. A simple per-query block kernel is unlikely to beat cuBLAS because cuBLAS leverages tiles and reuses K across many queries. But we can also tile across query positions. Now we need to write the kernel carefully. Key details: - D=128 -> D2=64 -> D2_PAD = 65. - BN=64 -> BN_PAD = 65. - BM=8 -> WARPS_PER_BLOCK = 8. - blockDim.x = 256. Inside the kernel: ```cpp template <typename T, int HEAD_SIZE> __global__ void fused_gqa_kernel( const T* __restrict__ q, const T* __restrict__ k, const T* __restrict__ v, T* __restrict__ out, int batch_size, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, float scale) ``` But we can specialize to HEAD_SIZE=128. Let's just use `constexpr int HEAD_SIZE = 128;` inside the kernel or template parameter. Actually we need to handle GQA: each query head maps to a kv_head. `kv_head = head / (num_heads / num_kv_heads)`. We can compute `int head = blockIdx.y; int kv_head = head * num_kv_heads / num_heads;` But integer division for each block is fine. Or precompute `int heads_per_kv = num_heads / num_kv_heads;` and `kv_head = head / heads_per_kv;`. Now, the data pointers. We need to offset correctly. Assuming T is `half` (for fp16). We'll dispatch from host based on dtype. Pointer offsets: - q_offset = ((batch * num_heads + head) * seq_len + query_global) * HEAD_SIZE + d0; - k_offset = ((batch * num_kv_heads + kv_head) * seq_len + tile_j + j) * HEAD_SIZE + d0; - v_offset similar. - out_offset similar to q. But d0 is lane*4. For loads, we load 4 halfs per thread. We can load as two `__half2`. Important: for K and V loads into smem, we do it for all threads in the block (256 threads). Each thread loads multiple __half2. We must ensure the loads are coalesced. Load K tile into smem: ```cpp extern __shared__ char smem_raw[]; __half2* smem_k = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(smem_raw); // layout: [BN][D2_PAD] for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < BN * D2_PAD; idx += blockDim.x) { int j = idx / D2_PAD; int d2 = idx % D2_PAD; if (d2 < D2) { int global_j = tile_j + j; if (global_j < seq_len) { int64_t offset = ((int64_t)batch * num_kv_heads + kv_head) * seq_len + global_j; offset = offset * HEAD_SIZE + d2 * 2; const __half2* src = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(k + offset); smem_k[j * D2_PAD + d2] = *src; } else { smem_k[j * D2_PAD + d2] = __floats2half2_rn(0.0f, 0.0f); } } } ``` But wait, each thread loads from K for a specific (j,d2). Consecutive threads have consecutive idx, so they load consecutive D2 elements for the same j, then next j. Since K row is D contiguous, and we load by iterating idx across j and d2, consecutive threads load consecutive elements. That's coalesced. Similarly for V. For Q load: each warp loads its own query. ```cpp float q_vals[4]; int query_global = block_i + warp_id; if (query_global < seq_len) { int64_t q_offset = ((int64_t)batch * num_heads + head) * seq_len + query_global; q_offset = q_offset * HEAD_SIZE + d0; const __half2* q_ptr2 = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(q + q_offset); __half2 q01 = q_ptr2[0]; __half2 q23 = q_ptr2[1]; q_vals[0] = __half2float(q01.x) * scale; q_vals[1] = __half2float(q01.y) * scale; q_vals[2] = __half2float(q23.x) * scale; q_vals[3] = __half2float(q23.y) * scale; } ``` Note: we scale Q here to avoid scaling later. For output write: ```cpp if (query_global < seq_len) { int64_t out_offset = ((int64_t)batch * num_heads + head) * seq_len + query_global; out_offset = out_offset * HEAD_SIZE + d0; __half2 o01 = __halves2half2(__float2half(acc[0]), __float2half(acc[1])); __half2 o23 = __halves2half2(__float2half(acc[2]), __float2half(acc[3])); reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(out + out_offset)[0] = o01; reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(out + out_offset)[1] = o23; } ``` Wait, we need to be careful about `__halves2half2` and `__float2half`. For performance, we can use `__float2half2_rn` etc. But `__float2half` is fine. Now, the score reduction: For each j in 0..BN-1: ```cpp float partial = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) { // Load K int d2 = (lane * 4 + d) / 2; int comp = (lane * 4 + d) % 2; // 0 for x, 1 for y __half2 k2 = smem_k[j * D2_PAD + d2]; float kf = (comp == 0) ? __half2float(k2.x) : __half2float(k2.y); partial += q_vals[d] * kf; } // warp reduce sum #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { partial += __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, partial, offset); } if (lane == 0) { smem_s[warp_id * BN_PAD + j] = partial; } __syncwarp(); // ensure all writes done? Actually lane 0 writes sequentially for j. ``` But this is slow because inside the j loop we do shuffle reduction. 64 shuffles per warp, each of 5 steps = 320 shuffles. That's okay. Alternatively, we could compute partial for each j and store in a local array, then do a parallel reduction using shared memory. But the shuffle is fine. But wait, the inner loop over d loads from smem_k. Since smem_k is 2D but we access `j * D2_PAD + d2`, which involves multiplication. The compiler can strength-reduce it. But we can compute the base pointer per j: `__half2* k_row = smem_k + j * D2_PAD;`. Better: ```cpp for (int j = 0; j < BN; ++j) { __half2* k_row = smem_k + j * D2_PAD; float partial = q_vals[0] * __half2float(k_row[lane*2].x) + q_vals[1] * __half2float(k_row[lane*2].y) + q_vals[2] * __half2float(k_row[lane*2+1].x) + q_vals[3] * __half2float(k_row[lane*2+1].y); // reduce ... } ``` This is more efficient. Now, for the local max pass: Each lane reads two scores from smem_s (jj = lane and lane+32): ```cpp float local_max = -INFINITY; #pragma unroll for (int idx = lane; idx < BN; idx += WARP_SIZE) { float s = smem_s[warp_id * BN_PAD + idx]; local_max = fmaxf(local_max, s); } // warp reduce max #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { local_max = fmaxf(local_max, __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_max, offset)); } float tile_max = __shfl_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_max, 0); ``` But note: the loop `for (int idx = lane; idx < BN; idx += WARP_SIZE)` with BN=64 will execute 2 iterations per thread. That's fine. Then update m and scale acc: ```cpp float m_new = fmaxf(m, tile_max); float scale_factor = expf(m - m_new); #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) acc[d] *= scale_factor; l *= scale_factor; m = m_new; ``` For tile_sum and accumulation: ```cpp float local_sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int idx = lane; idx < BN; idx += WARP_SIZE) { float s = smem_s[warp_id * BN_PAD + idx]; float p = expf(s - m_new); local_sum += p; __half2* v_row = smem_k + idx * D2_PAD; // smem_k reused for V acc[0] += p * __half2float(v_row[lane*2].x); acc[1] += p * __half2float(v_row[lane*2].y); acc[2] += p * __half2float(v_row[lane*2+1].x); acc[3] += p * __half2float(v_row[lane*2+1].y); } // warp reduce sum #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { local_sum += __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_sum, offset); } float tile_sum = __shfl_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_sum, 0); l += tile_sum; ``` Note: we reuse `smem_k` buffer for V. When loading V, `smem_k` pointer is overwritten. So `v_row` points to the same smem buffer. We need to be careful about the reuse: after computing scores, we do `__syncthreads()`, then load V into the smem buffer. Then `__syncthreads()`. Then the accumulation loop reads from smem buffer. Wait, we also need to load K and V with the same indexing pattern. Since we only have one smem buffer, we load K, compute scores (using K), store scores in smem_s. Then load V (overwriting smem buffer). Since all warps are done reading K after the score loop, the syncthreads ensures no read-after-write hazard. One subtlety: the score loop writes to smem_s. The max/accumulation loops read from smem_s. This is within the same warp. No need for syncthreads between them because the writes are by lane 0 and reads are by all threads in the same warp. Since a warp executes in lockstep, and lane 0 writes before other lanes read? In the score loop, lane 0 writes `smem_s[warp_id * BN_PAD + j]` for each j. After the loop, `__syncwarp()` ensures visibility. Then in max loop, all threads read smem_s. That's fine. But there is a potential race between warps: warp 0 writes to smem_s[0..63], warp 1 writes to smem_s[65..127] (since BN_PAD=65). Since they are in different rows of smem_s, and each row is separate, no bank conflict or race between warps. However, `__syncwarp()` is only within a warp. Between warps, no need to sync for smem_s because each warp only accesses its own row. But `__syncthreads()` is needed before loading V, because V load overwrites smem buffer. We need all warps to finish reading K. So: ``` __syncthreads(); // after K load score loop... __syncthreads(); // ensure all warps done with K before overwriting load V... __syncthreads(); // after V load accumulation loop... __syncthreads(); // before next K load (or end of tile) ``` Actually after accumulation, we don't need `__syncthreads()` before loading next K because the next iteration starts with loading K into smem buffer. But all threads must be done reading V before overwriting. However, the next tile's first step is loading K, which is done by all threads. If some threads are still reading V from previous tile, it's a hazard. But since all threads in the block participate in the loop and the accumulation loop ends at the same time for all threads (assuming same number of iterations), the compiler/hardware will ensure that before the next `__syncthreads()` (at the start of next tile's K load), all reads are done. So we need `__syncthreads()` at the top of the tile loop: after the previous tile's accumulation, before loading K for next tile. So: ``` for tile ... __syncthreads(); // ensure previous tile's V reads done load K ... __syncthreads(); compute scores ... __syncthreads(); // ensure all K reads done before overwriting load V ... __syncthreads(); accumulate ... ``` Wait, between loading K and compute scores we need syncthreads because threads load K and then all threads read it. Yes. Between compute scores and load V, we need syncthreads because V load overwrites K buffer and some warps might still be in the score loop reading K. But the score loop is done by all warps before we proceed. Since there's no `__syncthreads()` inside the per-warp score loop, warps might diverge: warp 0 may finish score loop and start loading V while warp 7 is still in score loop. That's a race! So we need a `__syncthreads()` after the score loop and before loading V. Similarly, after loading V, we need `__syncthreads()` before accumulation. And after accumulation, we need `__syncthreads()` before the next tile's load K. However, note that score loop writes to smem_s, which is separate from smem_k. So we can overlap loading V with score loop? No, because V load writes to smem_k. So must wait. So per tile: ```cpp __syncthreads(); // load K __syncthreads(); // score loop (each warp reads K, writes smem_s) __syncthreads(); // load V (overwrite smem_k) __syncthreads(); // accumulate (each warp reads smem_s and smem_k) // no syncthreads needed at end if loop continues and has syncthreads at top ``` But is the first `__syncthreads()` needed? At the start of the first iteration, there is no previous read. We can place `__syncthreads()` at the end of the loop. So: ```cpp for (int tile_j = 0; tile_j < seq_len; tile_j += BN) { // load K ... __syncthreads(); // score loop ... __syncthreads(); // load V ... __syncthreads(); // accumulate ... __syncthreads(); } ``` That's 4 syncthreads per tile. With 16 tiles, 64 syncthreads per block. On H100, syncthreads is fast but not free. 64 per block * 32768 blocks = 2M syncthreads. Fine. Wait, but `acc` scaling and `m` update happen between score and accumulation. We compute `tile_max`, scale acc, then load V. During load V, can we keep the CPU-like load happening while some warps compute tile_max? No, because V load is cooperative and requires `__syncthreads()` before and after. Let's draft the kernel carefully. ```cpp #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <c10/cuda/CUDAStream.h> #define WARP_SIZE 32 template <int HEAD_SIZE> __global__ void fused_gqa_kernel( const half* __restrict__ q, const half* __restrict__ k, const half* __restrict__ v, half* __restrict__ out, int batch_size, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, float scale) { constexpr int BN = 64; // keys per tile constexpr int BN_PAD = BN + 1; constexpr int BM = 8; // queries per block (warps per block) constexpr int D2 = HEAD_SIZE / 2; // __half2 per row constexpr int D2_PAD = D2 + 1; constexpr int D2_PER_THREAD = D2 / WARP_SIZE; extern __shared__ char smem_raw[]; __half2* smem_kv = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(smem_raw); float* smem_s = reinterpret_cast<float*>(smem_raw + BN * D2_PAD * sizeof(__half2)); int lane = threadIdx.x % WARP_SIZE; int warp_id = threadIdx.x / WARP_SIZE; int block_i = blockIdx.x * BM; int head = blockIdx.y; int batch = blockIdx.z; int query_global = block_i + warp_id; int heads_per_kv = num_heads / num_kv_heads; int kv_head = head / heads_per_kv; // Load Q into registers float q_reg[D2_PER_THREAD * 2]; bool valid = (query_global < seq_len); if (valid) { int64_t q_base = ((int64_t)batch * num_heads + head) * seq_len + query_global; const __half2* q_ptr2 = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(q + q_base * HEAD_SIZE); #pragma unroll for (int d2 = 0; d2 < D2_PER_THREAD; ++d2) { int d2_idx = lane * D2_PER_THREAD + d2; __half2 val = q_ptr2[d2_idx]; q_reg[d2 * 2 + 0] = __half2float(val.x) * scale; q_reg[d2 * 2 + 1] = __half2float(val.y) * scale; } } float m = -1e20f; // -inf float l = 0.0f; float acc[D2_PER_THREAD * 2]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < D2_PER_THREAD * 2; ++i) acc[i] = 0.0f; for (int tile_j = 0; tile_j < seq_len; tile_j += BN) { // Load K tile into smem_kv for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < BN * D2_PAD; idx += blockDim.x) { int j = idx / D2_PAD; int d2 = idx % D2_PAD; if (d2 < D2) { int global_j = tile_j + j; if (global_j < seq_len) { int64_t k_base = ((int64_t)batch * num_kv_heads + kv_head) * seq_len + global_j; const __half2* k_ptr2 = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(k + k_base * HEAD_SIZE); smem_kv[j * D2_PAD + d2] = k_ptr2[d2]; } else { smem_kv[j * D2_PAD + d2] = __float2half2_rn(0.0f); } } } __syncthreads(); if (valid) { // Compute scores for this tile #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < BN; ++j) { float partial = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int d2 = 0; d2 < D2_PER_THREAD; ++d2) { int d2_idx = lane * D2_PER_THREAD + d2; __half2 k2 = smem_kv[j * D2_PAD + d2_idx]; partial += q_reg[d2 * 2 + 0] * __half2float(k2.x); partial += q_reg[d2 * 2 + 1] * __half2float(k2.y); } // Warp reduce sum #pragma unroll for (int offset = WARP_SIZE / 2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { partial += __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, partial, offset); } if (lane == 0) { smem_s[warp_id * BN_PAD + j] = partial; } } __syncwarp(); // Find tile max float local_max = -1e20f; #pragma unroll for (int idx = lane; idx < BN; idx += WARP_SIZE) { float s = smem_s[warp_id * BN_PAD + idx]; local_max = fmaxf(local_max, s); } #pragma unroll for (int offset = WARP_SIZE / 2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { local_max = fmaxf(local_max, __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_max, offset)); } float tile_max = __shfl_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_max, 0); // Update running max and scale acc/l float m_new = fmaxf(m, tile_max); float scale_factor = expf(m - m_new); #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < D2_PER_THREAD * 2; ++i) acc[i] *= scale_factor; l *= scale_factor; m = m_new; } // else valid: just participate in syncthreads __syncthreads(); // Load V tile into smem_kv (reuse buffer) for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < BN * D2_PAD; idx += blockDim.x) { int j = idx / D2_PAD; int d2 = idx % D2_PAD; if (d2 < D2) { int global_j = tile_j + j; if (global_j < seq_len) { int64_t v_base = ((int64_t)batch * num_kv_heads + kv_head) * seq_len + global_j; const __half2* v_ptr2 = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(v + v_base * HEAD_SIZE); smem_kv[j * D2_PAD + d2] = v_ptr2[d2]; } else { smem_kv[j * D2_PAD + d2] = __float2half2_rn(0.0f); } } } __syncthreads(); if (valid) { float local_sum = 0.0f; // Each thread processes BN/WARP_SIZE keys (2 keys for BN=64) #pragma unroll for (int idx = lane; idx < BN; idx += WARP_SIZE) { float score = smem_s[warp_id * BN_PAD + idx]; float p = expf(score - m); local_sum += p; #pragma unroll for (int d2 = 0; d2 < D2_PER_THREAD; ++d2) { int d2_idx = lane * D2_PER_THREAD + d2; __half2 v2 = smem_kv[idx * D2_PAD + d2_idx]; acc[d2 * 2 + 0] += p * __half2float(v2.x); acc[d2 * 2 + 1] += p * __half2half2(v2.y); } } #pragma unroll for (int offset = WARP_SIZE / 2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { local_sum += __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_sum, offset); } float tile_sum = __shfl_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_sum, 0); l += tile_sum; } __syncthreads(); } // end for tile_j if (valid) { float inv_l = 1.0f / l; int64_t o_base = ((int64_t)batch * num_heads + head) * seq_len + query_global; __half2* out_ptr2 = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(out + o_base * HEAD_SIZE); #pragma unroll for (int d2 = 0; d2 < D2_PER_THREAD; ++d2) { int d2_idx = lane * D2_PER_THREAD + d2; float a = acc[d2 * 2 + 0] * inv_l; float b = acc[d2 * 2 + 1] * inv_l; out_ptr2[d2_idx] = __halves2half2(__float2half(a), __float2half(b)); } } } ``` Wait, I made a mistake in the accumulation: `__half2half2` is not a function. Now, the launcher: ```cpp void launch_fused_gqa(const half* q, const half* k, const half* v, half* out, int batch, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, int head_size, float scale) { constexpr int BN = 64; constexpr int BM = 8; dim3 grid((seq_len + BM - 1) / BM, num_heads, batch); dim3 block(BM * 32); int D2 = head_size / 2; int D2_PAD = D2 + 1; size_t smem_size = BN * D2_PAD * sizeof(__half2) + BM * (BN + 1) * sizeof(float); cudaStream_t stream = at::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream(); switch (head_size) { case 64: fused_gqa_kernel<64><<<grid, block, smem_size, stream>>>( q, k, v, out, batch, num_heads, num_kv_heads, seq_len, scale); break; case 128: fused_gqa_kernel<128><<<grid, block, smem_size, stream>>>( q, k, v, out, batch, num_heads, num_kv_heads, seq_len, scale); break; case 192: fused_gqa_kernel<192><<<grid, block, smem_size, stream>>>( q, k, v, out, batch, num_heads, num_kv_heads, seq_len, scale); break; case 256: fused_gqa_kernel<256><<<grid, block, smem_size, stream>>>( q, k, v, out, batch, num_heads, num_kv_heads, seq_len, scale); break; default: AT_ERROR("Unsupported head size for fused GQA: ", head_size); } } ``` Wait, we didn't pass `head_size` to the kernel because it's a template parameter. Now, potential correctness issues: - Floating point scale: `scale = head_size ** -0.5`. In our kernel, we multiply Q by scale during load. The hidden reference might compute `scores = Q @ K^T * scale`. Then softmax. Then output. Multiplying Q by scale is mathematically equivalent to scaling scores. Good. - Online softmax algorithm: we used the standard algorithm. Let's double-check the math. - `m_new = max(m, tile_max)`. - `scale_factor = exp(m - m_new)`. - `acc *= scale_factor`. - `l *= scale_factor`. - Then for each j in tile: `p = exp(score - m_new)`. - `acc += p * V_j`. - `tile_sum = sum(p)`. - `l += tile_sum`. - `m = m_new`. After all tiles: `out = acc / l`. This is correct. At any point, `acc` is the weighted sum of V for all processed keys, using unnormalized weights `exp(score - m_current)`. And `l` is the sum of those weights. When we update `m`, we rescale previous weights by `exp(m_old - m_new)`, which moves them to the new reference max, and then add new weights. This preserves the invariant that `acc = sum_{processed} exp(score - m) * V` and `l = sum_{processed} exp(score - m)`. At the end, dividing by `l` gives softmax-weighted average. Let's test with a small example mentally. Tile 1: scores = [1,2], m_new=2, p=[exp(-1), exp(0)]=[0.3679,1], acc = 0.3679*V1 + 1*V2, l=1.3679. Tile 2: scores=[3,0], tile_max=3, m_new=max(2,3)=3, scale=exp(2-3)=0.3679. acc*=0.3679 => 0.1353*V1 + 0.3679*V2. l*=0.3679 => 0.5032. New p: exp(3-3)=1, exp(0-3)=0.0498. acc += 1*V3 + 0.0498*V4 => 0.1353*V1 + 0.3679*V2 + 1*V3 + 0.0498*V4. l += 1 + 0.0498 = 1.553. m=3. True softmax weights for all scores [1,2,3,0]: Max = 3. exp([-2,-1,0,-3]) = [0.1353,0.3679,1,0.0498]. Sum = 1.553. Normalized weights = [0.0871, 0.2369, 0.6439, 0.0321]. Our acc / l = [0.1353/1.553, 0.3679/1.553, 1/1.553, 0.0498/1.553] = same as normalized weights. Yes! Dividing at the end: `inv_l = 1/l`, `acc * inv_l`. Correct. - The output matches reference implementation (which does standard softmax). Numerical differences: our online softmax accumulates in the same order as reference? Reference computes all scores, then softmax over entire dimension. Since we process tiles in order, the order is the same (j=0..S-1). The max and sum are computed in the same order (though online). For exact arithmetic, online softmax is equivalent. In floating point, there may be tiny differences due to rounding of intermediate scaling. But with fp16 output and 1e-2 tolerance, it should be fine. - Need to handle the case `seq_len` is not a multiple of BN (64). In our loops: - Load K: `if (global_j < seq_len) ... else zero`. The `torch.empty_like(q)` will be contiguous. `get_inputs` returns contiguous tensors. So we can assume contiguous. For safety, we could use stride. assistant```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline CUDA_SOURCE = r""" #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <c10/cuda/CUDAStream.h> #define WARP_SIZE 32 constexpr int BN = 64; // keys per tile constexpr int BN_PAD = BN + 1; constexpr int BM = 8; // queries per block (= warps per block) template <int HEAD_SIZE> __global__ void fused_gqa_kernel( const half* __restrict__ q, const half* __restrict__ k, const half* __restrict__ v, half* __restrict__ out, int batch_size, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, float scale) { constexpr int D2 = HEAD_SIZE / 2; // __half2 per row constexpr int D2_PAD = D2 + 1; constexpr int D2_PER_THREAD = D2 / WARP_SIZE; extern __shared__ char smem_raw[]; __half2* smem_kv = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(smem_raw); float* smem_s = reinterpret_cast<float*>(smem_raw + BN * D2_PAD * sizeof(__half2)); int lane = threadIdx.x % WARP_SIZE; int warp_id = threadIdx.x / WARP_SIZE; int block_i = blockIdx.x * BM; int head = blockIdx.y; int batch = blockIdx.z; int query_global = block_i + warp_id; int heads_per_kv = num_heads / num_kv_heads; int kv_head = head / heads_per_kv; // Registers for Q and accumulator float q_reg[D2_PER_THREAD * 2]; float acc[D2_PER_THREAD * 2]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < D2_PER_THREAD * 2; ++i) acc[i] = 0.0f; bool valid = (query_global < seq_len); if (valid) { int64_t q_off = ((int64_t)batch * num_heads + head) * seq_len + query_global; const __half2* q_ptr2 = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(q + q_off * HEAD_SIZE); #pragma unroll for (int d2 = 0; d2 < D2_PER_THREAD; ++d2) { int d2_idx = lane * D2_PER_THREAD + d2; __half2 val = q_ptr2[d2_idx]; q_reg[d2 * 2 + 0] = __half2float(val.x); q_reg[d2 * 2 + 1] = __half2float(val.y); } } float m = -1e20f; float l = 0.0f; for (int tile_j = 0; tile_j < seq_len; tile_j += BN) { int num_valid = (tile_j + BN <= seq_len) ? BN : (seq_len - tile_j); // ---- Load K tile into smem_kv ---- for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < BN * D2_PAD; idx += blockDim.x) { int j = idx / D2_PAD; int d2 = idx % D2_PAD; if (d2 < D2) { int global_j = tile_j + j; if (global_j < seq_len) { int64_t k_off = ((int64_t)batch * num_kv_heads + kv_head) * seq_len + global_j; const __half2* k_ptr2 = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(k + k_off * HEAD_SIZE); smem_kv[j * D2_PAD + d2] = k_ptr2[d2]; } else { smem_kv[j * D2_PAD + d2] = __float2half2_rn(0.0f); } } } __syncthreads(); // ---- Compute scores Q @ K^T for this tile ---- if (valid) { #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < num_valid; ++j) { float partial = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int d2 = 0; d2 < D2_PER_THREAD; ++d2) { int d2_idx = lane * D2_PER_THREAD + d2; __half2 k2 = smem_kv[j * D2_PAD + d2_idx]; partial += q_reg[d2 * 2 + 0] * __half2float(k2.x); partial += q_reg[d2 * 2 + 1] * __half2float(k2.y); } // Warp reduction sum #pragma unroll for (int offset = WARP_SIZE / 2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { partial += __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, partial, offset); } partial *= scale; if (lane == 0) { smem_s[warp_id * BN_PAD + j] = partial; } } // Pad remaining entries with -inf so they don't affect softmax if (lane == 0) { for (int j = num_valid; j < BN; ++j) { smem_s[warp_id * BN_PAD + j] = -1e20f; } } __syncwarp(); // ---- Tile max ---- float local_max = -1e20f; #pragma unroll for (int idx = lane; idx < BN; idx += WARP_SIZE) { if (idx < num_valid) { float s = smem_s[warp_id * BN_PAD + idx]; local_max = fmaxf(local_max, s); } } #pragma unroll for (int offset = WARP_SIZE / 2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { local_max = fmaxf(local_max, __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_max, offset)); } float tile_max = __shfl_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_max, 0); // ---- Update running softmax stats and scale acc ---- float m_new = fmaxf(m, tile_max); float scale_factor = expf(m - m_new); #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < D2_PER_THREAD * 2; ++i) acc[i] *= scale_factor; l *= scale_factor; m = m_new; } __syncthreads(); // ---- Load V tile into smem_kv (reuse buffer) ---- for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < BN * D2_PAD; idx += blockDim.x) { int j = idx / D2_PAD; int d2 = idx % D2_PAD; if (d2 < D2) { int global_j = tile_j + j; if (global_j < seq_len) { int64_t v_off = ((int64_t)batch * num_kv_heads + kv_head) * seq_len + global_j; const __half2* v_ptr2 = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(v + v_off * HEAD_SIZE); smem_kv[j * D2_PAD + d2] = v_ptr2[d2]; } else { smem_kv[j * D2_PAD + d2] = __float2half2_rn(0.0f); } } } __syncthreads(); // ---- Accumulate weighted V ---- if (valid) { float local_sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int idx = lane; idx < BN; idx += WARP_SIZE) { if (idx < num_valid) { float score = smem_s[warp_id * BN_PAD + idx]; float p = expf(score - m); local_sum += p; #pragma unroll for (int d2 = 0; d2 < D2_PER_THREAD; ++d2) { int d2_idx = lane * D2_PER_THREAD + d2; __half2 v2 = smem_kv[idx * D2_PAD + d2_idx]; acc[d2 * 2 + 0] += p * __half2float(v2.x); acc[d2 * 2 + 1] += p * __half2float(v2.y); } } } #pragma unroll for (int offset = WARP_SIZE / 2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { local_sum += __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_sum, offset); } float tile_sum = __shfl_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_sum, 0); l += tile_sum; } __syncthreads(); } // Write output if (valid) { float inv_l = 1.0f / l; int64_t o_off = ((int64_t)batch * num_heads + head) * seq_len + query_global; __half2* out_ptr2 = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(out + o_off * HEAD_SIZE); #pragma unroll for (int d2 = 0; d2 < D2_PER_THREAD; ++d2) { int d2_idx = lane * D2_PER_THREAD + d2; half h0 = __float2half_rn(acc[d2 * 2 + 0] * inv_l); half h1 = __float2half_rn(acc[d2 * 2 + 1] * inv_l); out_ptr2[d2_idx] = __halves2half2(h0, h1); } } } // Host launcher ---------------------------------------------------------- inline void launch_fused_gqa(const half* q_ptr, const half* k_ptr, const half* v_ptr, half* out_ptr, int batch, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, int head_size, float scale) { dim3 grid((seq_len + BM - 1) / BM, num_heads, batch); dim3 block(BM * WARP_SIZE); int D2 = head_size / 2; int D2_PAD = D2 + 1; size_t smem_size = BN * D2_PAD * sizeof(__half2) + BM * BN_PAD * sizeof(float); cudaStream_t stream = at::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream(); switch (head_size) { case 64: fused_gqa_kernel<64><<<grid, block, smem_size, stream>>>( q_ptr, k_ptr, v_ptr, out_ptr, batch, num_heads, num_kv_heads, seq_len, scale); break; case 128: fused_gqa_kernel<128><<<grid, block, smem_size, stream>>>( q_ptr, k_ptr, v_ptr, out_ptr, batch, num_heads, num_kv_heads, seq_len, scale); break; case 192: fused_gqa_kernel<192><<<grid, block, smem_size, stream>>>( q_ptr, k_ptr, v_ptr, out_ptr, batch, num_heads, num_kv_heads, seq_len, scale); break; case 256: fused_gqa_kernel<256><<<grid, block, smem_size, stream>>>( q_ptr, k_ptr, v_ptr, out_ptr, batch, num_heads, num_kv_heads, seq_len, scale); break; default: AT_ERROR("fused_gqa: unsupported head_size ", head_size); } } // PyTorch op wrapper ----------------------------------------------------- torch::Tensor fused_gqa_forward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v, int64_t num_kv_heads, double scale) { TORCH_CHECK(q.is_cuda(), "q must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(k.is_cuda(), "k must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(v.is_cuda(), "v must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(q.scalar_type() == at::kHalf, "q must be fp16"); TORCH_CHECK(k.scalar_type() == at::kHalf, "k must be fp16"); TORCH_CHECK(v.scalar_type() == at::kHalf, "v must be fp16"); int batch = q.size(0); int num_heads = q.size(1); int seq_len = q.size(2); int head_size = q.size(3); TORCH_CHECK(k.size(0) == batch && v.size(0) == batch); TORCH_CHECK(k.size(1) == num_kv_heads && v.size(1) == num_kv_heads); TORCH_CHECK(k.size(2) == seq_len && v.size(2) == seq_len); TORCH_CHECK(k.size(3) == head_size && v.size(3) == head_size); auto out = torch::empty_like(q); launch_fused_gqa( q.data_ptr<at::Half>(), k.data_ptr<at::Half>(), v.data_ptr<at::Half>(), out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), batch, num_heads, static_cast<int>(num_kv_heads), seq_len, head_size, static_cast<float>(scale) ); return out; } PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) { m.def("forward", &fused_gqa_forward, "Fused GQA forward"); } """ class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size): super().__init__() self.num_heads = num_heads self.num_kv_heads = num_kv_heads self.head_size = head_size self.scale = float(head_size) ** -0.5 self._mod = load_inline( name="fused_gqa", cpp_sources="", cuda_sources=CUDA_SOURCE, extra_cuda_cflags=["-O3", "--use_fast_math"], verbose=False, ) def forward(self, q, k, v): return self._mod.forward(q, k, v, self.num_kv_heads, self.scale) ```

i < D2_PER_THREAD * 2; ++i) acc[i] = 0.0f;\n\n bool valid = (query_global < seq_len);\n if (valid) {\n int64_t q_off = ((int64_t)batch * num_heads + head) * seq_len + query_global;\n const __half2* q_ptr2 = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(q + q_off * HEAD_SIZE);\n #pragma unroll\n for (int d2 = 0; d2 < D2_PER_THREAD; ++d2) {\n int d2_idx = lane * D2_PER_THREAD + d2;\n __half2 val = q_ptr2[d2_idx];\n q_reg[d2 * 2 + 0] = __half2float(val.x);\n q_reg[d2 * 2 + 1] = __half2float(val.y);\n }\n }\n\n float m = -1e20f;\n float l = 0.0f;\n\n for (int tile_j = 0; tile_j < seq_len; tile_j += BN) {\n int num_valid = (tile_j + BN <= seq_len) ? reasoning
The compile failed because of type incompatibility between c10::Half and half/CUDA's __half. I need to use the proper type casting. In PyTorch inline CUDA, `at::Half` is `c10::Half`, not directly CUDA's `half`. I should use `reinterpret_cast` or use `c10::Half` in the signature and cast it to `half*`/`const half*`. Actually, the simplest fix is to: 1. Use `at::Half*` in the kernel signatures (not `half*`), then inside cast to `__half*` if needed. 2. Or just use `reinterpret_cast<half*>(q.data_ptr<at::Half>())` etc. Let me fix the types. I'll use `const at::Half*` in the kernel signature and reinterpret_cast inside, or use `const void*` or simply cast at the call site. The most robust way is to define the kernels taking pointers as `const at::Half*` and cast them to `const __half*` inside. Or I could just use `reinterpret_cast<const half*>` at the call site. Let me update the code.

That's a 4-way bank conflict. But it might not be catastrophic. Actually, I just want to verify the algorithm more carefully: For tile t with scores S_t = {s_{t,0}, s_{t,1}, ..., s_{t,m-1}}: After processing tiles 0..t-1: - m = M_{t-1} (max of all scores seen so far) - l = L_{t-1} = sum_{j in tiles 0..t-1} exp(s_j - M_{t-1}) - acc = sum_{j in tiles 0..t-1} exp(s_j - M_{t-1}) * V_j Processing tile t: - m_t = max(M_{t-1}, max(S_t)) - For previous contributions: - scale = exp(M_{t-1} - m_t) - acc *= scale - l *= scale - For new contributions: - p_{t,j} = exp(s_{t,j} - m_t) - acc += sum_j p_{t,j} * V_{t,j} - l += sum_j p_{t,j} - Update m = m_t Final: - out = acc / l This is mathematically correct. If each warp stores scores at `smem_s[warp_id * BN + j]`, then adjacent warps write to `warp_id * BN + j` and `(warp_id + 1) * BN + j`. These are separated by BN floats. For BN=64, this is 64 * 4 = 256 bytes. On H100, 256 bytes is divisible by 32 bank widths (4 bytes each), so 256 / 4 = 64 banks... wait, 256 bytes maps to 64 consecutive banks. But since there are only 32 banks, it wraps around. So warp w's scores start at bank 0, and warp w+1's scores also start at bank 0. This could cause bank conflicts if two warps try to access the same bank simultaneously. But since each warp only accesses its own region, and the regions are 256 bytes apart, and 256 / 4 = 64, the offsets differ by 64 elements. 64 mod 32 = 0. So yes, warp w and warp w+1 would have the same bank for the same j. But since each warp's threads access their own warp's scores, there shouldn't be cross-warp conflicts during the same instruction. Unless there's divergence... Actually, no bank conflicts across warps because warps execute independently. Bank conflicts only happen within a warp when multiple threads in the same warp access the same bank. So as long as threads in a warp access different banks, there's no conflict. For warp w, thread lane accesses `smem_s[warp_id * BN + idx]`. For idx = lane, lane+32, etc.: lane=0: offset = w*64 + 0. bank = (w*64*4 + 0) % 128 / 4? Actually, bank = (byte_offset / 4) % 32. byte_offset = (w * BN + idx) * sizeof(float) = (w * 64 + idx) * 4 bank = (w * 64 + idx) % 32 = (w * 0 + idx) % 32 (since 64 % 32 = 0) = idx % 32 For idx = lane + k*32: bank = lane % 32. So all threads in a warp access different banks (0 through 31). No bank conflicts! Great. So I can use BN instead of BN_PAD. And for K/V shared memory, using HEAD_SIZE as the row stride: byte_offset = (j * HEAD_SIZE + d) * sizeof(half) = (j * 128 + d) * 2 bank = (j * 128 + d) / 2 % 32 = (j * 64 + d/2) % 32 For consecutive d (e.g., d=0,1,2,3 accessed by threads 0,1,2,3): Thread 0: d=0, bank = (j*64 + 0) % 32 = 0 Thread 1: d=1, bank = (j*64 + 0) % 32 = 0 (since d/2 = 0 for both d=0 and d=1) Thread 2: d=2, bank = (j*64 + 1) % 32 = 1 Thread 3: d=3, bank = (j*64 + 1) % 32 = 1 So threads 0 and 1 conflict, threads 2 and 3 conflict, etc. This is a 2-way bank conflict! To avoid this, I'd need HEAD_SIZE/2 to be odd or specifically chosen. HEAD_SIZE = 128, so HEAD_SIZE/2 = 64. And 64 % 32 = 0. So pairs of consecutive d values map to the same bank. This is a problem. I could add padding to K/V storage: `smem_k[j * (HEAD_SIZE + PAD) + d]` If PAD = 2, then row size = 130 halfs = 260 bytes. bank = (j * 130 + d) / 2 % 32 = (j * 65 + d/2) % 32 For d=0: j*65 % 32 = j % 32 (since 65 % 32 = 1) For d=1: same bank as d=0 (since d/2 = 0) For d=2: j*65 + 1 % 32 = (j + 1) % 32 So consecutive d values still pair up on the same bank. Hmm, this is tricky with `half` precision because adjacent half values (2 bytes each) always share a bank if the row byte size is divisible by 4. Wait, for 2-byte accesses, there's a special rule in recent GPUs: consecutive 2-byte accesses to consecutive addresses by consecutive threads go to different banks or get broadcast depending on the architecture. On Ampere and later, shared memory has 32 banks of 4 bytes. Two consecutive 2-byte accesses from consecutive threads to addresses B and B+2: if B % 4 == 0, then thread 0 accesses bank B/4, thread 1 accesses bank B/4 (same bank!) - this is a 2-way conflict. Process K/V in tiles of BN=64 6. Store scores in shared memory per warp 7. Online softmax 8. Write output Let me compute the elements per thread for Q loading: head_size = 128, warp_size = 32. So each thread loads 128/32 = 4 half values. I'll store them as `float q_reg[4];` For score computation with K tile: For each key j in tile (0 to num_valid-1): - Each thread computes partial = sum_{d=0..3} q_reg[d] * float(K[j, lane*4 + d]) - Warp reduce partial - Lane 0 stores score This is very clean. For V accumulation: For each key idx in tile that this thread processes: - p = exp(score - m) - local_sum += p - For d=0..3: acc[d] += p * float(V[idx, lane*4 + d]) Each thread handles keys idx = lane, lane+32, lane+64, ... After all tiles: - inv_l = 1/l - For d=0..3: out[query, lane*4 + d] = half(acc[d] * inv_l) This is very natural and avoids the `__half2` complexity. One subtlety: when loading K tile into shared memory, I need to make sure the layout is `[BN][HEAD_SIZE]` so that `smem_k[j * HEAD_SIZE + d]` gives key j, dimension d. Then thread lane accesses dimensions `lane*4` to `lane*4+3`. For K loading: ```cpp for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < BN * HEAD_SIZE; idx += blockDim.x) { int j = idx / HEAD_SIZE; int d = idx % HEAD_SIZE; int global_j = tile_j + j; half val; if (global_j < seq_len) { int64_t k_off = ((int64_t)batch * num_kv_heads + kv_head) * seq_len + global_j; val = k[k_off * HEAD_SIZE + d]; } else { val = __float2half(0.0f); } smem_k[j * HEAD_SIZE + d] = val; } ``` All threads participate. Good. Wait, but this might not be coalesced because consecutive threads could access very different addresses. Let me check: Thread 0: idx = 0, j=0, d=0 → k[k_off * 128 + 0] Thread 1: idx = 1, j=0, d=1 → k[k_off * 128 + 1] Thread 2: idx = 2, j=0, d=2 → k[k_off * 128 + 2] ... Thread 127: idx = 127, j=0, d=127 → k[k_off * 128 + 127] Thread 128: idx = 128, j=1, d=0 → k[(k_off+1) * 128 + 0] Wait, idx = j * HEAD_SIZE + d. For consecutive threads, if they all access the same key j, they access consecutive dimensions d. This is coalesced because consecutive threads access consecutive memory addresses. For thread 127: idx=127, j=0, d=127. For thread 128: idx=128, j=1, d=0. These are NOT consecutive addresses. Thread 127 accesses address base+127*2, thread 128 accesses (base+stride)+0. If k_off+1 is the next key, the address is offset by seq_len * HEAD_SIZE * sizeof(half) from the previous key's start. This is a large jump and not coalesced with thread 127. But threads 128-255 (if block size is 256) access key j=1, dimensions 0-127. These are coalesced among themselves but not with threads 0-127. Since coalescing happens within warps, and warp 0 is threads 0-31, warp 1 is threads 32-63, etc., let me check warp-level coalescing: Warp 0 (threads 0-31): accesses j=0, d=0-31. Consecutive, coalesced. Warp 1 (threads 32-63): accesses j=0, d=32-63. Consecutive, coalesced. Warp 2 (threads 64-95): accesses j=0, d=64-95. Consecutive, coalesced. Warp 3 (threads 96-127): accesses j=0, d=96-127. Consecutive, coalesced. Warp 4 (threads 128-159): accesses j=1, d=0-31. Consecutive, coalesced. Warp 5 (threads 160-191): accesses j=1, d=32-63. Consecutive, coalesced. Warp 6 (threads 192-223): accesses j=1, d=64-95. Consecutive, coalesced. Warp 7 (threads 224-255): accesses j=1, d=96-127. Consecutive, coalesced. So each warp does coalesced accesses. The issue is that warp 4 accesses a different key than warp 0, but since they're in different warps, the hardware handles this. Actually, this is fine because modern GPUs can handle multiple coalesced accesses from different warps. The only concern is that warps 0-3 and warps 4-7 access different cache lines, but this is normal. Great, the loading pattern is good. Now let me write the new kernel. I'll be very explicit and careful. Wait, I should also think about whether `__float2half` is the right function to use. For writing 0s to invalid positions, `__float2half(0.0f)` gives zero half. Correct. And `__half2float` converts half to float. Correct. One concern: `__half2float` might be slow if called many times, but correctness matters more. OK, let me write the new kernel now. I'll also change the module name to ensure we get a fresh compilation. I'll also handle the case where grid.x * BM > seq_len by zeroing out the extra output elements.

But the I/O shows v is just a standard tensor. Let me try another approach: check if the source code gives hints about what the hidden reference does. The source is paged_attention_v2. But the hidden reference might just be the standard `paged_attention_v2` PyTorch op from vLLM, applied to full sequence length. Actually, looking at the source more carefully: the comment says "Adapted from ... decoder_masked_multihead_attention_template.hpp". The function is called `paged_attention_v2_launcher` and `paged_attention_v2`. The I/O contract says: ```python def get_inputs(): q = torch.randn(BATCH, NUM_HEADS, SEQ_LEN, HEAD_SIZE, dtype=torch.float16).cuda() k = torch.randn(BATCH, NUM_KV_HEADS, SEQ_LEN, HEAD_SIZE, dtype=torch.float16).cuda() v = torch.randn(BATCH, NUM_KV_HEADS, SEQ_LEN, HEAD_SIZE, dtype=torch.float16).cuda() ``` This definitely looks like standard Q, K, V dense tensors, with GQA. The hidden reference might just be a standard PyTorch attention. Given that my kernel passes compilation but fails correctness, and the differences are ~3.5 max / 0.075 avg, I suspect either: 1. There's a bug in my kernel (most likely) 2. The reference expects causal masking Let me look for bugs one more time... Actually! I just found a very subtle bug! Let me trace through the warp shuffle for the dot product: ```cpp for (int offset = WARP_SIZE / 2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { partial += __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, partial, offset); } ``` After this, lane 0 has the sum. But what about `lane != 0`? They also have partial sums, but only lane 0 writes to shared memory. That's fine because only lane 0 is supposed to write. But then in the tile_max computation: ```cpp for (int idx = lane; idx < BN; idx += WARP_SIZE) { if (idx < num_valid) { float s = smem_s[warp_id * BN + idx]; local_max = fmaxf(local_max, s); } } ``` Here, ALL lanes in the warp read from `smem_s` and compute their own `local_max`, then do warp reduction. After the reduction, ALL lanes get the same `tile_max` via `__shfl_sync`. This is correct. Wait... hmm, let me look at the `__syncwarp()` after writing scores: ```cpp if (lane == 0) { smem_s[warp_id * BN + j] = partial; } ... __syncwarp(); ``` This ensures all threads in the warp can see the writes. Correct. Then tile_max reduction: ```cpp for (int offset = WARP_SIZE / 2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { local_max = fmaxf(local_max, __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_max, offset)); } float tile_max = __shfl_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_max, 0); ``` Wait! `__shfl_down_sync` only shifts values to lower lane IDs. After the first offset=16, lane 0..15 get fmaxf of lanes 0..15 and 16..31. After offset=8, lane 0..7 get fmaxf of 0..7 and 8..15, etc. Finally lane 0 has the max of all lanes. Then `__shfl_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_max, 0)` broadcasts lane 0's value to all lanes. This is correct. Wait, actually I realize there might be an issue with the `__shfl_down_sync` in the first reduction (dot product sum): ```cpp for (int offset = WARP_SIZE / 2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { partial += __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, partial, offset); } ``` Here, lane X reads from lane X + offset. For offset=16, lane 0 reads lane 16, lane 1 reads lane 17, ..., lane 15 reads lane 31. Then in the next iteration offset=8, lane 0..15 read from lane 8..23. This is correct. After all iterations, lane 0 has the sum. Now for the second reduction (local_sum): ```cpp for (int offset = WARP_SIZE / 2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { local_sum += __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_sum, offset); } ``` Same pattern, correct. So the warp reductions seem fine. Let me look at another potential bug. In the output write: ```cpp float inv_l = 1.0f / l; ``` What if `l` is 0? This shouldn't happen because at least one tile has valid entries. Wait, what if `seq_len` is 0? Not possible given the I/O. Hmm, let me reconsider. What if the issue is with the tile-based max tracking when there are many tiles? Standard online softmax: Initialize m = -inf, l = 0 For each tile: 1. Find tile_max (max score in tile) 2. m_new = max(m, tile_max) 3. Scale previous accumulator: acc *= exp(m - m_new), l *= exp(m - m_new) 4. For each valid score in tile: p = exp(score - m_new), acc += p * V, l += p 5. m = m_new This is correct. But wait, in my code I do: ```cpp float m_new = fmaxf(m, tile_max); float scale_factor = expf(m - m_new); ... m = m_new; ``` And then later for V: ```cpp float p = expf(score - m); ``` Wait! After I update m = m_new, when I compute `expf(score - m)` in the V loop, `m` is now `m_new`, so `score - m = score - m_new`. This is correct. But wait! Let me look more carefully. In the V loop, I do: ```cpp float score = smem_s[warp_id * BN + idx]; float p = expf(score - m); ``` But `smem_s` stores `score` which was already scaled by `scale`. And `m` is the running max. So `p = exp(score - m_new)` is correct for the current tile. However, the tile scores in `smem_s` were computed using the ORIGINAL `q_reg` values. But `q_reg` is never modified. So the scores are correct. Let me think about whether there could be a synchronization issue between warps. When lane 0 writes `smem_s[warp_id * BN + j]`, does any other warp read from that location? The tile_max computation only reads `smem_s[warp_id * BN + idx]` by the same warp (same warp_id). So no inter-warp contention. And the V accumulation also only reads `smem_s[warp_id * BN + idx]` by the same warp. So no contention. And `smem_kv` is loaded cooperatively by all threads, and then all warps read from it. After `__syncthreads()`, all threads can safely read. That's correct. Hmm, what about the very first iteration when reading Q? All warps read their own query. They don't share Q values. That's fine. Actually, wait a second. Each warp computes scores for its own query position using `q_reg`. Then for V accumulation, each warp uses its own `smem_s` row and accumulates into its own `acc`. This is correct. OK, I'm starting to think that maybe the issue is not a correctness bug but a precision issue. Let me think about what PyTorch's matmul does differently... In PyTorch, when you do `torch.matmul(q, k.transpose(-2, -1))` with fp16 inputs: - It might use cuBLAS with Tensor Cores - The default math mode on H100 for fp16 matmul might accumulate in fp16 or tf32 depending on flags - But typically, fp16 matmul uses fp32 accumulation in cuBLAS My manual fp32 accumulation should match or exceed cuBLAS fp32 accumulation. But there's a key difference: my dot product accumulates 128 products one by one in fp32. cuBLAS might split the 128-dim vector into chunks and use different reduction trees, or it might use warp-level matrix multiply (WMMA) instructions that accumulate in different ways. Still, the difference for a dot product of 128 elements shouldn't be huge. Let me try a completely different approach. What if I use a kernel that's much closer to the reference? Instead of a custom tiled kernel, I can use `F.scaled_dot_product_attention` internally but wrap it in a ModelNew class. That would definitely be correct but wouldn't be faster than the reference. Wait, the problem says "Write a custom CUDA kernel that replaces a PyTorch reference, producing numerically equivalent output faster than the reference." So I DO need a custom kernel, and it needs to be faster. What if I try to compute GQA using explicit K/V expansion (repeat_interleave) and then call a tensor-core friendly matmul? But that would require allocating intermediate tensors. Actually, let me think about this differently. Maybe the bug is in my indexing when computing the output. Let me re-examine the output write: ```cpp int64_t o_off = ((int64_t)batch * num_heads + head) * seq_len + q_pos; at::Half* o_ptr = out + o_off * HEAD_SIZE; for (int d = 0; d < ELEM_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o_ptr[lane * ELEM_PER_THREAD + d] = __float2half(acc[d] * inv_l); } ``` Wait, `o_ptr` points to the head vector for output position q_pos. Then lane X writes elements `lane * ELEM_PER_THREAD + d` for d=0..3. This is correct. But what about the output initialization? I use `torch::zeros_like(q)` in the wrapper, so all outputs start at 0. For valid threads, they write their values. For invalid threads (where q_pos >= seq_len), they don't write anything, so the output remains 0. But there shouldn't be invalid outputs because `grid.x = (seq_len + BM - 1) / BM`, and the last block might have some warps with q_pos >= seq_len. If seq_len is not divisible by BM, then the last block has some invalid warps. This means query position i only attends to positions 0..i. If the reference is causal and my code isn't, then: - For query position 0, my code attends to all 1024 positions, but reference attends to just 1. Massive difference. - For query position 512, my code attends to 1024 positions, reference to 512. Let me rewrite the kernel with causal masking... Actually, another consideration: for GQA, each query head attends to the same KV head. Causal masking is per-sequence-position, not per-head. So q_pos and global_j are sequence positions, independent of head. Let me implement this. This should fix the mismatch if the reference is indeed causal. Also, looking back at the original source: `decoder_masked_multihead_attention_template.hpp`. For decoder inference, each new token attends to all previous tokens (causal). But wait, the inputs have q of shape (batch, num_heads, seq_len, head_size), which has full seq_len queries. In training or in prefix prefill, you process all positions at once with causal masking. So yes, causal masking is very likely expected!

In my kernel, I compute: ```c++ float m_new = fmaxf(m, tile_max); float scale_factor = expf(m - m_new); ``` If `m = -1e20f` initially and `tile_max` is around -10 (a reasonable score), then `m_new = -10`, `scale_factor = exp(-1e20 - (-10)) = 0`. So the accumulators get multiplied by 0. Then `l *= 0 = 0`. Then `l += tile_sum`. So after the first tile: - `m = tile_max` - `l = tile_sum` - `acc = 0 + p * v (after rescaling)`. Wait, actually when `m = -1e20f` and the tile_max is, say, 3.0: - `m_new = 3.0` - `scale_factor = exp(-1e20 - 3.0) = 0` - `acc *= 0` (no change since acc is already 0) - `l *= 0 = 0` - `m = 3.0` Then in the V accumulation of the first tile: - For each score, `p = exp(score - m)` where `m = 3.0` - For the max score (score = 3.0), `p = exp(0) = 1` - For other scores, `p = exp(score - 3.0)` - `l += sum(p)` - `acc += p * v` After the first tile, `l` is the sum of exp-normalized probabilities for that tile. So `l` starts at 0 and becomes positive. That's correct. Now for the next tile, if tile_max = 2.0: - `m_new = max(3.0, 2.0) = 3.0` - `scale_factor = exp(3.0 - 3.0) = 1` - `acc *= 1` (unchanged) - `l *= 1` (unchanged) - `m = 3.0` (unchanged) Then V accumulation for the second tile: - `p = exp(score - 3.0) = exp(2.0 - 3.0) = exp(-1.0) ≈ 0.368` - `l += sum(p)` (adds the new probabilities) - `acc += p * v` (adds the weighted values) This is correct online softmax. Wait, what if the first tile's max is, say, -10 and the second tile's max is 5? - After first tile: `m = -10`, `l = sum(exp(score - (-10)))` - Second tile: `m_new = max(-10, 5) = 5` - `scale_factor = exp(-10 - 5) = exp(-15) ≈ 3.06e-7` - `acc *= 3.06e-7` - `l *= 3.06e-7` - `m = 5` Then V accumulation for second tile uses `p = exp(score - 5)`. This rescaled all previous contributions by a tiny factor. This is mathematically correct but numerically, if the first tile had significant contributions, rescaling them by exp(-15) will effectively make them 0. This is the standard issue with softmax numerical stability. In practice, for attention, the score distribution is usually concentrated near the max, so this is generally fine. No problem there. Actually, wait. Let me re-examine if there's a SMEM bank conflict issue. My smem_kv is accessed as `smem_kv[j * HEAD_SIZE + d]`. With HEAD_SIZE=128, adjacent threads in a warp access adjacent elements in the d dimension. When d varies by thread, like `d_idx = lane * 4 + d`, adjacent lanes access elements 4 apart. With 32 banks on A100/H100, each bank holds 4 bytes (for 32-bit mode) or the banking is based on 4-byte words. Actually, in newer GPUs, each bank is 4 bytes. With `at::Half` (2 bytes), `smem_kv[j * 128 + d_idx]`: - Thread 0 accesses d_idx=0 → address 0 - Thread 1 accesses d_idx=4 → address 8 - Thread 2 accesses d_idx=8 → address 16 - ... - Thread 8 accesses d_idx=32 → address 64 With 32 banks of 4 bytes each, addresses mod 128 determine the bank (since 32 banks * 4 bytes = 128 bytes stride for wrap-around). - Address 0 → bank 0 - Address 8 → bank 2 - Address 16 → bank 4 - Address 64 → bank 16 All threads access different banks! No bank conflicts. Good. What if the issue is caused by the fact that I'm doing online softmax but with a slightly different order? Let me look at the log-sum-exp formulation more carefully. At the end of processing all tiles: l = Σ exp(score_i - m_final) acc = Σ exp(score_i - m_final) * v_i Then output = acc / l. Is it possible that the reference computes: output = softmax(scores) @ v Where `softmax(scores)_i = exp(score_i) / Σ exp(score_j)` ? These are mathematically equivalent: acc / l = Σ exp(score_i - m) * v_i / Σ exp(score_j - m) = Σ exp(score_i) * exp(-m) * v_i / (Σ exp(score_j) * exp(-m)) = Σ exp(score_i) * v_i / Σ exp(score_j) Yes, the exp(-m) cancels! At the end, output = acc / l gives the proper probability-weighted average. During the second access (d=1), thread 0 accesses base+2 (still bank 0), thread 1 accesses base+6 (bank 1), etc. Still different banks. So no bank conflicts. In my current kernel: ```c++ float tile_sum = __shfl_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, local_sum, 0); l += tile_sum; ``` `local_sum` is the sum of p over keys in the tile handled by this thread (or warp, via `__shfl_down_sync`). Actually, the partitioning in vLLM should be mathematically equivalent to a single online softmax if done correctly. The only differences would come from floating point rounding in the partition boundaries. For online softmax, the order of processing keys matters due to floating point associativity. If keys are processed in order 0, 1, 2, ..., 1023, the intermediate max and rescaling happen in that order. In the partition approach, keys 0-511 are processed first (finding m0), then keys 512-1023 (finding m1), then combined. This is a different grouping but the final online softmax formula is applied. Wait, I think I see a potential issue with vLLM's partition approach. In vLLM, each partition computes partial attention output. The partial output is computed using the local max and local sum within that partition. When combining partitions, the reduce kernel rescales by the global max. But the PARTIAL OUTPUT is: `acc_partition = Σ_j∈partition exp(score_j - m_partition) * v_j` And `l_partition = Σ_j∈partition exp(score_j - m_partition)` Then when combining: `acc_total = acc_p0 * exp(m_p0 - m_global) + acc_p1 * exp(m_p1 - m_global) + ...` `l_total = l_p0 * exp(m_p0 - m_global) + l_p1 * exp(m_p1 - m_global) + ...` `output = acc_total / l_total` This should be mathematically equivalent to single-pass online softmax. The only difference is that intermediate results are rescaled by partition max instead of running max. In exact arithmetic, it's the same. So differences would only come from floating point rounding, which should be small. Given all this, I'm very confused about the ~3.5 difference. For small tiles, I could load K and V into registers. With BN=128 and each thread loading 4 elements (128/32=4), we can load 128 keys from global memory directly. Actually, for K loading without shared memory, each thread could load 4 dimensions of K for each key. But then for the dot product, we need the same key for all threads. We'd have to load K sequentially: for each key j, threads load K[j][lane*4 + d], compute partial products, shuffle to get score. This works without any shared memory! And since there are no shared memory writes, no `__syncthreads()` is needed. Let me write this zero-SMEM version! For a warp handling one query position: 1. Each thread loads 4 elements of Q into registers. 2. Initialize acc[4] = 0, m = -inf, l = 0. 3. Loop over keys j = 0 to seq_len-1: a. Each thread loads K[j][lane*4 + d] from global memory. b. Compute partial dot product. c. Warp shuffle to get full score. d. Update online softmax. e. Each thread loads V[j][lane*4 + d] from global memory. f. Compute p = exp(score - m_new) and accumulate into acc and l. 4. Write output. Wait, for step 3d and 3e, we need to load V AFTER computing the score and updating m. But at that point, m might have changed. So: For each j: - Load K[j] - Compute score_j - m_new = max(m, score_j) - factor = exp(m - m_new) - acc *= factor - l *= factor - m = m_new - p = exp(score_j - m) - Load V[j] - acc += p * V[j] - l += p But `l += p` should only happen once per key, not once per thread. Because l is the sum of probabilities over all keys. Since there are 32 threads, if each thread adds p to l, we'd count p 32 times. So we need to add p only once per key. With the warp approach, we can have lane 0 maintain l: ```c++ if (lane == 0) { l += p; } // Then broadcast l to all threads? Or all threads maintain l independently // by having lane 0 compute and broadcast l = __shfl_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, l, 0); ``` Wait, `l` needs to be updated correctly. Let me think about how to handle `l`: Option A: Only lane 0 maintains `l`. After each key, lane 0 updates `l` and broadcasts it. Option B: All threads maintain `l` independently. After computing `p = exp(score - m)`, we add `p` to `l` only on one thread, then broadcast. Option A is the cleanest: ```c++ float l = 0.0f; // Only lane 0 uses this, but all threads declare it float m = -1e20f; // All threads have the same m for (int j = 0; ... ) { // Compute score float score = ...; // Same for all threads after shuffle float m_new = fmaxf(m, score); float factor = expf(m - m_new); for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) acc[d] *= factor; m = m_new; if (lane == 0) { l *= factor; } float p = expf(score - m); if (lane == 0) { l += p; } // Broadcast l to all threads float l_broadcast = __shfl_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, l, 0); // Load V[j][lane*4+d] // acc[d] += p * v[d] } ``` Actually, all threads need `l` at the end for normalization. But during the loop, only the acc rescaling needs `l` if we're doing something with it. Wait, in classic online softmax, `l` is only needed at the end to normalize. During the loop, we only rescale `acc` and `l` by the factor, and add new terms. The intermediate `l` value within the loop doesn't affect `acc` unless we're doing something else. So we can let lane 0 maintain `l`, and other threads don't need it during the loop. At the end, lane 0's `l` is broadcast for normalization. Let me rewrite more carefully: ```c++ float q_reg[4]; // load Q float acc[4] = {0, 0, 0, 0}; float m = -1e20f; float l = 0.0f; // Only valid for lane 0, but declare everywhere for (int j = 0; j < seq_len; ++j) { // Compute score_j float partial = 0.0f; for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) { float k_val = __half2float(k[...]); partial += q_reg[d] * k_val; } // Warp reduce #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) { partial += __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, partial, offset); } float score = __shfl_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, partial, 0) * scale; // Online softmax update float m_new = fmaxf(m, score); float factor = expf(m - m_new); #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) acc[d] *= factor; m = m_new; if (lane == 0) { l = l * factor + expf(score - m); } // Load V[j] and accumulate float p = expf(score - m); for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) { float v_val = __half2float(v[...]); acc[d] += p * v_val; } } if (lane == 0) { l = __shfl_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, l, 0); } float inv_l = 1.0f / l; // Write output ``` Wait, in the `l` update: `l = l * factor + expf(score - m)`. Since `m = m_new`, `expf(score - m) = expf(score - m_new)`. And `factor = expf(m_old - m_new)`. So: `l_new = l_old * expf(m_old - m_new) + expf(score - m_new)` If score is the new max: `l_new = l_old * expf(m_old - score) + 1` If score <= old max: `l_new = l_old + expf(score - m_old)` This is the correct online softmax l update. In my code above, I put `l` update inside `if (lane == 0)`. That's fine. At the end, I broadcast `l`. But notice that all threads compute `p = expf(score - m)` and load V and accumulate. Even though `score` and `m` are the same for all threads (I broadcast score to all threads using `__shfl_sync`), `p` is the same for all threads. This is fine. But wait, I need to broadcast `m` too! Actually, `m` is updated the same way on all threads, so it's always consistent. After `m = m_new;`, all threads have the same `m`. And `score` is broadcast via `__shfl_sync`. So all threads compute the same `factor` and `p`. This zero-SMEM kernel is very simple and should be easy to verify. Let me write it! Actually, one potential issue: global memory access pattern for K and V. For K[j][lane*4 + d]: ```c++ int64_t k_off = ((int64_t)batch * num_kv_heads + kv_head) * seq_len + j; const at::Half* k_ptr = k + k_off * HEAD_SIZE; float k_val = __half2float(k_ptr[lane * 4 + d]); ``` In a warp, thread lane reads `k_ptr[lane * 4 + d]`. For d=0: - lane 0: k_ptr[0] - lane 1: k_ptr[4] - lane 2: k_ptr[8] - ... - lane 31: k_ptr[124] These are spaced 4 halfs = 8 bytes apart. Global memory coalescing prefers consecutive accesses. With 8-byte stride, this is not fully coalesced. In fact, consecutive threads access bytes that are 8 apart. A warp accesses 32 * 2 = 64 bytes total, but they're spread across 256 consecutive bytes (from byte 0 to byte 252). On H100, a warp can read 32 bytes per transaction. So we'd need 8 transactions (256/32 = 8). Not ideal, but not catastrophic. We can improve this by having threads read consecutive elements and then shuffle: - Thread 0 reads k_ptr[0], k_ptr[1], k_ptr[2], k_ptr[3] - Thread 1 reads k_ptr[4], k_ptr[5], k_ptr[6], k_ptr[7] - etc. This is the same pattern. To get fully coalesced access: - Thread 0 reads k_ptr[0..3] - Thread 1 reads k_ptr[4..7] - ... Actually, consecutive threads reading consecutive 4-element chunks IS coalesced! Because: - Thread 0 accesses bytes 0..7 (4 halfs) - Thread 1 accesses bytes 8..15 - Thread 2 accesses bytes 16..23 - ... - Thread 31 accesses bytes 248..255 These ARE consecutive in memory and can be merged into a single 256-byte transaction. Modern GPUs can merge accesses from all threads in a warp into fewer transactions as long as the accessed bytes are within a cache line and aligned. For K[j], the base address `k_ptr` is aligned to 128 bytes if the tensor is properly aligned (which PyTorch ensures). So a warp reading 256 bytes from an aligned 128-byte boundary is very efficient. Great, so the access pattern is actually good! But wait, I need to read 4 elements per thread, and I do it in a loop over d. The compiler might unroll this, but the accesses are strided within each thread. Thread 0 accesses bytes 0..7 (4 accesses). If I write it as: ```c++ for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) { k_val[d] = __half2float(k_ptr[lane * 4 + d]); } ``` Thread 0 accesses: 0, 2, 4, 6 bytes. Thread 1 accesses: 8, 10, 12, 14 bytes. These are interleaved within the 256-byte range. The GPU might still merge them into a single transaction or a few transactions. Alternatively, I could load using `__half2` to read 2 halfs at once: ```c__ __half2 k2 = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(k_ptr)[lane * 2]; k_val[0] = __half2float(k2.x); k_val[1] = __half2float(k2.y); __half2 k2_2 = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(k_ptr)[lane * 2 + 1]; k_val[2] = __half2float(k2_2.x); k_val[3] = __half2float(k2_2.y); ``` This is much better! Each thread loads two `__half2` values (4 halfs total). Thread lane loads `__half2` at indices `lane*2` and `lane*2+1`. Since `__half2` is 4 bytes, thread lane accesses bytes `lane*8` and `lane*8+4`. Thread 0: bytes 0, 4. Thread 1: bytes 8, 12. Thread 2: bytes 16, 20. These are 4 bytes apart. Actually, `reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(k_ptr)[lane * 2]` accesses address `k_ptr + lane * 8`. `lane * 8` is the byte offset. For a warp: - lane 0: offset 0, 4 - lane 1: offset 8, 12 - lane 2: offset 16, 20 - ... - lane 31: offset 248, 252 These accesses span bytes 0..255. In 4-byte accesses, a warp can make 64 individual 4-byte accesses. The GPU's L1 cache can combine these into fewer transactions based on cache line (128 bytes). So 2 transactions for the whole warp. Very efficient! Let me use this pattern. Alright, I'll write the zero-SMEM kernel. This eliminates any possibility of shared memory bugs. It should be functionally correct if the math is right. One more thing: `__shfl_down_sync` for warp reduction of the dot product. ```c++ float partial = 0.0f; for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) { partial += q_reg[d] * k_val[d]; } #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) { partial += __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, partial, offset); } float score = __shfl_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, partial, 0) * scale; ``` This gives the same score to all threads. Then online softmax and V accumulation as outlined. This approach avoids ALL shared memory, ALL `__syncthreads()`, and is very explicit. If it still gives the ~3.5 error, then the issue is definitely fundamental (different expected computation), not a kernel bug. Actually, wait. I don't want to run out of turns. I have 3 turns and 20 tool calls left. I can compile and test this new kernel in 2 turns. If it fails again, I might need to try something else or just submit. Let me write this kernel and compile it. Wait, one thing with the zero-SMEM approach is performance. Each warp reads K[j] and V[j] directly from global memory for each j. This is roughly 2x more global memory bandwidth than using shared memory (since each warp loads its own copy). But for correctness, we don't care. Actually, in the current implementation with shared memory, each block of 4 warps loads ONE K tile and ONE V tile, which are shared among the 4 warps. This uses 4x less memory bandwidth than having each warp load individually. But it also introduces the shared memory complexity that might be causing issues. If the zero-SMEM kernel passes, then the bug was indeed in shared memory handling! Let me code it up. Actually wait, one subtlety with memory loading. For K and V, the tensor layout in PyTorch for a tensor of shape [B, H, T, D] is row-major. The last dimension D is contiguous. So K[b][h][t][:] is a contiguous vector of D elements. My indexing `k_off * HEAD_SIZE + d` accesses the d-th element of the t-th key. This is correct. The `__half2` loads assume alignment to 4 bytes. For `HEAD_SIZE=128`, `k_off * 128 * 2` bytes is `k_off * 256` bytes. If `k_off` is an integer, then `k_off * 256` is divisible by 256, so certainly divisible by 4. The `__half2` loads will be aligned. Let me code this zero-SMEM kernel now.

Actually in my causal shared memory kernel, the bug might be that I do `__syncthreads()` after computing scores and updating m, but before the V loading. All warps in a block need to agree on `m` before loading V. But each warp computes its own `m` independently! In my causal kernel: ```cpp if (q_pos >= tile_j) { float m_new = fmaxf(m, tile_max); // ... m = m_new; } __syncthreads(); ``` The problem is that different warps (handling different q_pos) might have different `m` values. Then when loading V, they all use their own `m`. Wait, could there be a bank conflict issue with smem_s? `smem_s[warp_id * BN + idx]` - different warps access different rows, but within a warp, threads with consecutive `lane` values access consecutive elements. With `BN=64`, and `warp_id` ranging 0-7, the addressing is: - Thread lane accesses `smem_s[warp_id * 64 + lane]`, `smem_s[warp_id * 64 + lane + 32]` Since `smem_s` is float (4 bytes), consecutive lanes access consecutive 4-byte words, which maps to consecutive banks. So there's no bank conflict for the score array. For `smem_kv`, threads access `smem_kv[idx * HEAD_SIZE + d_idx]`. With `HEAD_SIZE=128`, and `idx` varying, the stride is 128*2=256 bytes (since at::Half is 2 bytes). This maps to: - bank = (idx * HEAD_SIZE + d_idx) % 32 - For fixed `d_idx` (which depends on lane), threads with different `idx` access banks: (idx * 128 + lane*4) % 32 Since 128 % 32 = 0, this becomes `(lane * 4) % 32`. Consecutive lanes access banks 0, 4, 8, 12, 16, 20, 24, 28, 0, 4, 8... So there are bank conflicts! Specifically, groups of 8 consecutive lanes access the same bank. But bank conflicts slow things down, they don't cause incorrect results. Actually wait, I realize this is becoming too focused on performance. The correctness is the issue. This is correct because all keys in this tile (64..127) are > 5, so they're all masked out. If q_pos = 70 and tile_j = 64, then q_pos >= tile_j, so we process the tile. Let me look at the score computation in the causal kernel more carefully: ```cpp for (int j = 0; j < num_valid; ++j) { int global_j = tile_j + j; float score; if (global_j > q_pos) { score = -1e20f; } else { // compute dot product score = partial * scale; } if (lane == 0) { smem_s[warp_id * BN + j] = score; } } ``` Here, if `global_j > q_pos`, we set `score = -1e20f` unconditionally in smem_s. But in the tile max computation, we skip entries where `global_j > q_pos`. So `tile_max` doesn't see the `-1e20f` values. Good. But then in the accumulation: ```cpp for (int idx = lane; idx < BN; idx += WARP_SIZE) { int global_j = tile_j + idx; if (idx < num_valid && global_j <= q_pos) { float score = smem_s[warp_id * BN + idx]; float p = expf(score - m); // acc and l } } ``` Only entries with `global_j <= q_pos` are accumulated. The scores for these entries are the correct dot products. Good. So the causal kernel seems correct. Why doesn't it match? One possibility: there's a race condition. After computing scores, `__syncwarp()` is used. But `__syncwarp()` only synchronizes threads within the SAME warp. However, the score computation writes to `smem_s`, and the max computation reads from `smem_s`. Since all threads in a warp participate in both writing and reading, `__syncwarp()` should be sufficient. But wait - `__syncwarp()` requires ALL threads in the warp to call it. In my code: ```cpp if (lane == 0) { for (int j = num_valid; j < BN; ++j) { smem_s[warp_id * BN + j] = -1e20f; } } __syncwarp(); ``` After the for loop, ALL threads in the warp reach `__syncwarp()`. Thread 0 has finished writing the padding. Other threads have no writes. Then all threads in the warp sync. So thread 1, which immediately proceeds to the max computation, sees the padding written by thread 0. Good. But what if `__syncwarp()` is placed outside the `if (valid)` block? In the causal kernel: ```cpp if (valid) { // compute scores // write padding __syncwarp(); // compute max } __syncthreads(); ``` All threads in a warp call `__syncwarp()` (because valid is true for all threads in the warp if q_pos < seq_len). But what if q_pos >= seq_len? Then `valid = false`, and no threads in the warp call `__syncwarp()`. Is that a problem? If valid is false, the warp handles a query position beyond seq_len, so it doesn't participate in any computation. The issue is whether other warps in the same block need data from this warp. They don't, because each warp only accesses its own row of smem_s. So if a warp is inactive, it doesn't matter. But actually, `__syncthreads()` after the score block: ```cpp if (valid) { // compute scores and max } __syncthreads(); ``` `__syncthreads()` must be called by ALL threads in the block. Since I have it outside the `if (valid)` block, ALL threads call it. Good. Then the warps with `q_pos < tile_j` skip the V loading and go straight to `__syncthreads()`. The warps with `q_pos >= tile_j` load V and then hit `__syncthreads()`. Since `__syncthreads()` is reached by all threads, this is fine. But wait - the V loading OVERWRITES the smem_kv buffer with V data. The score computation previously used smem_kv to hold K data. for (int d = 0; d < ELEM_PER_THREAD; ++d) acc[d] = 0.0f; float m = -1e20f; float l = 0.0f; for (int j = 0; j < seq_len; ++j) { // Load K[j] float k_reg[ELEM_PER_THREAD]; int64_t k_off = ((int64_t)batch * num_kv_heads + kv_head) * seq_len + j; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { partial += __shfl_down_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, partial, offset); } float score = __shfl_sync(0xFFFFFFFF, partial, 0) * scale; // Online softmax float m_new = fmaxf(m, score); float factor = expf(m - m_new); if (lane == 0) { l = l * factor + expf(score - m_new); } for (int d = 0; d < ELEM_PER_THREAD; ++d) { acc[d] *= factor; } m = m_new; // Load V[j] float v_reg[ELEM_PER_THREAD]; int64_t v_off = ((int64_t)batch * num_kv_heads + kv_head) * seq_len + j; float inv_l = 1.0f / l; int64_t o_off = ((int64_t)batch * num_heads + head) * seq_len + q_pos; __half2* out_ptr2 = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(out + o_off * HEAD_SIZE); for (int d2 = 0; d2 < ELEM_PER_THREAD / 2; ++d2) { int idx = lane * (ELEM_PER_THREAD / 2) + d2; half h0 = __float2half_rn(acc[d2 * 2 + 0] * inv_l); half h1 = __float2half_rn(acc[d2 * 2 + 1] * inv_l); out_ptr2[idx] = __halves2half2(h0, h1); } } ``` I just noticed something interesting. The online softmax update in my kernel: ```cpp float m_new = fmaxf(m, score); float factor = expf(m - m_new); if (lane == 0) { l = l * factor + expf(score - m_new); } // ... acc *= factor ... m = m_new; ``` And then: ```cpp float p = expf(score - m); ``` Since m = m_new now, and score <= m_new: - If score == m_new (the new max), then p = exp(0) = 1 - If score < m_new, then p = exp(score - m_new) < 1 And `l` gets updated as: - l_new = l_old * factor + exp(score - m_new) - If score == m_new: l_new = l_old * exp(m_old - m_new) + 1 - If score < m_new: l_new = l_old * exp(m_old - m_new) + exp(score - m_new) This is the standard online softmax algorithm and is correct. Wait, but I realize the `l` update uses `expf(score - m_new)` where `score` is the CURRENT score, not the max. But if score == m_new, then exp(score - m_new) = 1. But the contribution to l for this specific score should be 1 (representing the unnormalized probability for the new max). Actually let me think. In standard softmax: ``` L = sum_i exp(x_i - m) where m = max(x_i) ``` After processing the first element x_0: ``` m = x_0 l = exp(x_0 - x_0) = 1 acc = v_0 ``` After processing x_1 where x_1 > x_0: ``` m_new = x_1 factor = exp(x_0 - x_1) l = 1 * factor + exp(x_1 - x_1) = exp(x_0 - x_1) + 1 acc = v_0 * factor + v_1 ``` After processing x_2 where x_2 < x_1: ``` m_new = x_1 (unchanged) factor = exp(x_1 - x_1) = 1 l = (exp(x_0 - x_1) + 1) * 1 + exp(x_2 - x_1) = exp(x_0 - x_1) + 1 + exp(x_2 - x_1) acc = (v_0 * factor_01 + v_1) * 1 + v_2 * exp(x_2 - x_1) = v_0 * exp(x_0 - x_1) + v_1 + v_2 * exp(x_2 - x_1) ``` And finally: ``` out = acc / l = [v_0 * exp(x_0 - x_1) + v_1 + v_2 * exp(x_2 - x_1)] / [exp(x_0 - x_1) + 1 + exp(x_2 - x_1)] ``` This equals: ``` [v_0 * exp(x_0) / exp(x_1) + v_1 + v_2 * exp(x_2) / exp(x_1)] * exp(x_1) / [exp(x_0) + exp(x_1) + exp(x_2)] = [v_0 * exp(x_0) + v_1 * exp(x_1) + v_2 * exp(x_2)] / [exp(x_0) + exp(x_1) + exp(x_2)] ``` which is the correct softmax-weighted average!
